       84  \section{Preference Elicitation Beyond Recommendation Dialogue
           }
       85  \label{sec:elicitation_llm}
       86  
       48 -Preference elicitation has a research history older than CRS, 
          -rooted in decision theory and Bayesian methods \citep{viappian
          -i2009regret, mangili2020bayesian, christakopoulou2016towards},
          - and the LLM era has revived it outside the CRS mainline in fo
          -rms relevant to this thesis. GATE \citep{li2023eliciting} demo
          -nstrates that LLMs can themselves conduct free-form preference
          - interviews — generating open questions, edge cases, and examp
          -le queries — and that the elicited information improves downst
          -ream task performance over user-written specifications; howeve
          -r, GATE's questioning is generated greedily by prompting, with
          - no learned strategy and no optimisation target tied to a reco
          -mmender. PEBOL \citep{austin2024bayesian} frames natural-langu
          -age preference elicitation as Bayesian optimisation: a belief 
          -over item utilities is maintained, and the LLM serves as a nat
          -ural-language acquisition function proposing the next query un
          -der expected-improvement-style criteria. This is the most prin
          -cipled treatment of \textit{what to ask next} in the recent li
          -terature, but it is deliberately myopic (one-step acquisition 
          -rather than a learned multi-turn policy) and operates over ite
          -m descriptions rather than an open concept space. In the CRS c
          -ontext proper, \citet{kostric2024generating} generate \textit{
          -usage-oriented} elicitation questions — asking how an item wil
          -l be used rather than which attributes are desired — reflectin
          -g a broader shift toward elicitation grounded in user context 
          -rather than catalogue schemata, and \citet{kostric2025should} 
          -show experimentally that the conversational \textit{style} of 
          -elicitation measurably affects user satisfaction and elicitati
          -on success, with adaptive, flexible styles performing best.   
       87 +Preference elicitation has a research history older than CRS, 
          +rooted in decision theory, where the object of inquiry is a ut
          +ility function and questions are chosen to reduce uncertainty 
          +about it — whether by minimax regret over the remaining feasib
          +le utilities \citep{viappiani2009regret}, by Bayesian updating
          + of a belief over user models \citep{mangili2020bayesian}, or 
          +by bandit-style absolute and comparative questions about items
          + \citep{christakopoulou2016towards}. The LLM era has revived t
          +his tradition in forms directly relevant to this thesis, becau
          +se the language model removes what was previously the binding 
          +constraint: the need to confine questions to forms a system co
          +uld both generate and interpret.                              
       88  
       50 -Taken together, this strand confirms both halves of the thesis
          - premise: elicitation quality measurably matters and is active
          -ly studied, yet none of these works \textit{learns a multi-tur
          -n questioning strategy} — GATE and usage-question generation a
          -re unoptimised generation, PEBOL is single-step Bayesian acqui
          -sition, and the style work concerns the form rather than the c
          -ontent of questions. The learned-strategy tradition surveyed i
          -n Section~\ref{sec:rl_lineage} and the LLM-era elicitation wor
          -k surveyed here have, as of this writing, intersected only at 
          -the coarse strategy-type level \citep{zhao2025reinforced, deng
          -2023plug}.                                                    
       89 +GATE \citep{li2023eliciting} demonstrates that LLMs can themse
          +lves conduct free-form preference interviews. In their framewo
          +rk, the language model elicits a user's intended task specific
          +ation through interaction — generating open-ended questions, p
          +robing edge cases, and proposing example inputs for the user t
          +o judge — across domains that include content recommendation, 
          +and the elicited specifications improve downstream decisions o
          +ver both user-written prompts and labelled examples, while oft
          +en requiring less user effort. GATE is, in effect, an existenc
          +e proof that LLMs can \textit{conduct} elicitation; what it de
          +liberately does not provide is a learned strategy — its questi
          +oning is generated greedily by prompting, with no optimisation
          + target tied to a downstream recommender and no notion of mult
          +i-turn planning. PEBOL \citep{austin2024bayesian} supplies the
          + principled decision-theoretic treatment that GATE lacks, fram
          +ing natural-language preference elicitation as Bayesian optimi
          +sation: the system maintains a probabilistic belief over the u
          +tilities of catalogue items, and at each turn an acquisition s
          +trategy in the style of Thompson sampling or upper-confidence 
          +selection identifies the item description about which a yes/no
          + preference query would be most valuable, with the LLM serving
          + to verbalise the query and to map the user's natural-language
          + answer back into the belief update via entailment-style infer
          +ence. PEBOL outperforms monolithic prompted-GPT elicitation, w
          +hich is itself notable as a direct, controlled demonstration t
          +hat adding explicit decision-theoretic structure on top of an 
          +LLM beats trusting the LLM's own conversational instincts. Its
          + limitations mirror its strengths: the acquisition is delibera
          +tely myopic — a one-step criterion rather than a learned multi
          +-turn policy — and it operates over item-level descriptions ra
          +ther than an open concept space.                              
       90  
       91 +Within the CRS mainline, the elicitation question itself has c
          +ome under study as an object of design. \citet{kostric2024gene
          +rating} generate \textit{usage-oriented} elicitation questions
          + — asking how or in what context an item will be used, rather 
          +than which attributes are desired — on the grounds that users 
          +with limited domain expertise can answer usage questions more 
          +reliably than schema questions; their generation pipeline mine
          +s item reviews for usage signals, and the work reflects a broa
          +der shift toward elicitation grounded in user context rather t
          +han catalogue schemata. Complementing the question-content per
          +spective, \citet{kostric2025should} examine the conversational
          + \textit{style} of elicitation, contrasting a fast-paced, dire
          +ct, high-involvement style with a patient, accommodating, high
          +-considerateness style in a controlled user study, and find th
          +at style measurably affects satisfaction and elicitation effec
          +tiveness, with the ability to adapt style to the user performi
          +ng best. Both results underline that elicitation quality is mu
          +lti-dimensional; both also concern dimensions orthogonal to th
          +e one this thesis optimises, namely the strategic selection of
          + what to ask next.                                            
       92 +                                                              
       93 +Taken together, this strand confirms both halves of the thesis
          + premise: elicitation quality measurably matters and is active
          +ly studied, yet none of these works \textit{learns a multi-tur
          +n questioning strategy} — GATE and usage-question generation a
          +re unoptimised generation, PEBOL is single-step Bayesian acqui
          +sition, and the style work concerns the form rather than the c
          +ontent of questions. The learned-strategy tradition surveyed i
          +n Section~\ref{sec:rl_lineage} and the LLM-era elicitation wor
          +k surveyed here have, as of this writing, intersected only at 
          +the coarse strategy-type level \citep{zhao2025reinforced, deng
          +2023plug}; the content-level intersection remains open.       
       94 +                                                              
       95  \section{Evaluation Methodology in the LLM Era}
       96  \label{sec:eval_norms}
       97  
       55 -Evaluation practice has shifted alongside system architecture.
          - The use of LLMs as user simulators, introduced systematically
          - by \citet{wang2023rethinking}, has become the dominant protoc
          -ol for interactive evaluation of CRS, replacing both static di
          -alogue replay and the rigid rule-based simulators of the discr
          -ete-action lineage \citep{lei2020estimation, deng2021unified}.
          - The advantages are realism of language and the ability to sim
          -ulate persona-conditioned behaviour from interaction histories
          -; the risks, acknowledged but not yet resolved in the literatu
          -re, include simulator--reality divergence, systematic biases o
          -f the simulating model, and the circularity that arises when t
          -he same model family simulates the user, generates system utte
          -rances, and judges quality. This thesis's evaluation design — 
          -simulated users instantiated from held-out MovieLens profiles 
          -\citep{harper2015movielens}, with the simulator constrained to
          - answer from the profile's ground-truth ratings — sits between
          - the rule-based and free-LLM extremes, trading some linguistic
          - naturalism for verifiable consistency between simulated state
          -ments and ground truth, which is essential when the measured q
          -uantity is information gain.                                  
       98 +\subsection{User Simulation}                                  
       99  
       57 -A further observation from this review is methodological: whil
          -e recommendation accuracy benchmarks abound, there is no estab
          -lished benchmark or agreed protocol for measuring \textit{stra
          -tegic elicitation ability} in isolation — that is, how much re
          -commendation-relevant information a system's questions extract
          - per turn, holding the recommender and simulator fixed. Publis
          -hed comparisons embed elicitation inside whole-system evaluati
          -ons where its effect is confounded with recommendation and gen
          -eration quality. The diagnostic experiments conducted in this 
          -thesis (Chapter~\ref{ch:con}) found, consistent with this, tha
          -t commonly used reward signals for elicitation, such as per-tu
          -rn gains in ranking metrics over small candidate pools, can be
          - statistically too noisy to support either learning or fair co
          -mparison. The absence of such a protocol is itself a gap this 
          -thesis is positioned to address, and grounds the evaluation-fi
          -rst contribution proposed in the concluding chapter.          
      100 +Evaluation practice has shifted alongside system architecture.
          + The discrete-action lineage of Section~\ref{sec:rl_lineage} e
          +valuated against rule-based simulators that answer attribute q
          +ueries truthfully from a held-out profile and accept a recomme
          +ndation exactly when it matches ground truth \citep{lei2020est
          +imation, deng2021unified} — fully verifiable, but linguistical
          +ly empty and behaviourally rigid. The use of LLMs as user simu
          +lators, introduced systematically by \citet{wang2023rethinking
          +}, has since become the dominant protocol for interactive eval
          +uation, offering natural language, persona conditioning, and v
          +aried behaviour. The risks, acknowledged but not yet resolved 
          +in the literature, are equally real: the simulator may diverge
          + from human behaviour in unknown ways; its underlying model ca
          +rries biases (toward popular items, toward agreeableness) that
          + interact with the system under test; its knowledge of the ful
          +l catalogue can leak information a real user would not provide
          +; and a circularity arises when the same model family simulate
          +s the user, generates the system's utterances, and judges the 
          +outcome. Simulator design has thus become part of the experime
          +ntal design, and results are only as interpretable as the simu
          +lator is controlled.                                          
      101  
      102 +This thesis's evaluation design — simulated users instantiated
          + from held-out MovieLens profiles \citep{harper2015movielens},
          + with the simulator constrained to answer from the profile's g
          +round-truth ratings, optionally through tool-mediated lookups 
          +that make every simulated statement auditable against the prof
          +ile — sits deliberately between the rule-based and free-LLM ex
          +tremes. It trades some linguistic naturalism for verifiable co
          +nsistency between simulated statements and ground truth, which
          + is essential when the measured quantity is information gain: 
          +an unconstrained simulator that hallucinates a preference inje
          +cts unmeasurable noise into precisely the signal under study. 
      103 +                                                              
      104 +\subsection{Measuring Elicitation, and a Methodological Gap}  
      105 +                                                              
      106 +A further observation from this review is methodological. Whil
          +e recommendation accuracy metrics are thoroughly standardised 
          +— NDCG, MRR, hit rate at fixed cut-offs, success rate and aver
          +age turns for multi-round dialogues — there is no established 
          +benchmark or agreed protocol for measuring \textit{strategic e
          +licitation ability} in isolation: that is, how much recommenda
          +tion-relevant information a system's questions extract per tur
          +n, holding the recommender, the simulator, and the candidate p
          +ool fixed. Published comparisons embed elicitation inside whol
          +e-system evaluations, where its effect is confounded with the 
          +quality of recommendation, generation, and language understand
          +ing; a system may score well because it asks good questions, o
          +r because its recommender squeezes more from the same answers,
          + and the published metrics cannot tell these apart. The confou
          +nd matters practically as well as conceptually, because the ca
          +ndidate policies now in play are of radically different kinds 
          +— graph-pruned Q-networks, Bayesian acquisition, prompted LLMs
          +, policy-plus-verbaliser hybrids — and the field has no instru
          +ment for comparing them on equal terms.                       
      107 +                                                              
      108 +The diagnostic experiments conducted in this thesis (Chapter~\
          +ref{ch:con}) add a quantitative warning to this picture: commo
          +nly used reward signals for elicitation, such as per-turn chan
          +ges in ranking metrics over modest candidate pools, can be sta
          +tistically too noisy to support either learning or fair compar
          +ison — in this thesis's measurements, per-turn metric deltas w
          +ere an order of magnitude smaller than their per-user standard
          + deviation under realistic pool sizes and evaluation filters. 
          +Any evaluation (or training) protocol for elicitation must the
          +refore be designed around the signal-to-noise ratio of its mea
          +surement instrument, a consideration absent from the published
          + evaluations reviewed here. The absence of such a protocol is 
          +itself a gap this thesis is positioned to address, and grounds
          + the evaluation-first contribution proposed in Chapter~\ref{ch
          +:con}.

      138  
      139  The gap claimed in earlier versions of this work — that no exi
           sting system combines reinforcement learning in continuous act
           ion spaces, LLM-based question generation, and preference elic
           itation — survives the verification conducted for this chapter
           , but only when stated as a claim about the \textit{combinatio
           n}, with each constituent honestly attributed. Continuous-acti
           on reinforcement learning with nearest-neighbour grounding is 
           the Wolpertinger architecture \citep{dulacarnold2015deep}, dem
           onstrated on recommendation-scale action sets; continuous cont
           rol has been applied to sequential recommendation \citep{wang2
           024efficient}; the policy-chooses/LLM-speaks decomposition exi
           sts at the strategy-type level \citep{deng2023plug, zhao2025re
           inforced}; and LLMs both generate elicitation questions \citep
           {li2023eliciting, kostric2024generating} and learn, at the tok
           en level, to ask clarifying questions \citep{zhang2025modeling
           }. What no verified work does is learn a multi-turn elicitatio
           n policy whose actions are points in a continuous \textit{sema
           ntic} space — decoupling the policy's decision (``what concept
            to probe next'') from both the catalogue schema that constrai
           ns the discrete lineage and the closed strategy inventories of
            RSO and PPDPP — grounded to interpretable entities and verbal
           ised by an LLM. Equally, no verified work provides a controlle
           d protocol for measuring strategic elicitation ability across 
           approaches of such different kinds; the lineage systems, the p
           rompt-based LLM systems, and the Bayesian systems have never b
           een compared on equal terms with the recommender, simulator, a
           nd evaluation pool held fixed.
      140  
       89 -This thesis accordingly advances two contributions in its rema
          -ining chapters. The first is methodological: a controlled eval
          -uation framework for strategic preference elicitation, in whic
          -h a fixed, polarity-aware recommendation model serves as the m
          -easurement instrument, simulated users answer verifiably from 
          -held-out ground truth, and elicitation policies of heterogeneo
          -us types — random and popularity baselines, greedy information
          --gain, prompt-only LLM questioning, and learned policies inclu
          -ding the published bot-play approach \citep{makarova2024learni
          -ng} — are compared on information gained per conversational tu
          -rn. The second is algorithmic: the extension of the bot-play f
          -ramework to a continuous semantic action space with LLM verbal
          -isation, evaluated within that framework against the strongest
          - non-learned and LLM-based baselines. The diagnostic findings 
          -already obtained (Chapter~\ref{ch:con}) — concerning reward si
          -gnal-to-noise ratios, the necessity of separating preference p
          -olarity from semantic content in user-state representations, a
          -nd the sample-efficiency demands of continuous-action learning
          - — directly inform the design of both contributions.          
      141 +These two gaps jointly define the research questions for the r
          +emainder of this thesis:                                      
      142 +                                                              
      143 +\begin{itemize}                                               
      144 +    \item \textbf{RQ1 (measurement).} How can strategic prefer
          +ence elicitation be measured in isolation — with the recommend
          +ation model, user simulator, and candidate pool held fixed — s
          +uch that policies of heterogeneous kinds (random, information-
          +theoretic, prompted-LLM, and learned) can be compared on equal
          + terms, and what signal-to-noise properties must the measureme
          +nt instrument satisfy for such comparisons to be statistically
          + meaningful?                                                  
      145 +    \item \textbf{RQ2 (the LLM baseline).} Under such a protoc
          +ol, how strategically do prompt-only LLM questioners actually 
          +behave relative to principled non-learned baselines such as gr
          +eedy information gain — quantifying, rather than asserting, th
          +e elicitation weakness attributed to LLMs in the literature?  
      146 +    \item \textbf{RQ3 (the learned policy).} Does a multi-turn
          + policy learned in a continuous semantic action space, grounde
          +d to entities and verbalised by an LLM, outperform both the di
          +screte formulation of \citet{makarova2024learning} and the str
          +ongest baselines from RQ2 — and under what training regime (sa
          +mple budget, reward signal, grounding strategy) does the conti
          +nuous formulation become learnable at all?                    
      147 +\end{itemize}                                                 
      148 +                                                              
      149 +This thesis accordingly advances two contributions in its rema
          +ining chapters. The first is methodological, answering RQ1 and
          + RQ2: a controlled evaluation framework for strategic preferen
          +ce elicitation, in which a fixed, polarity-aware recommendatio
          +n model serves as the measurement instrument, simulated users 
          +answer verifiably from held-out ground truth, and elicitation 
          +policies of heterogeneous types — random and popularity baseli
          +nes, greedy information-gain, prompt-only LLM questioning, and
          + learned policies including the published bot-play approach \c
          +itep{makarova2024learning} — are compared on information gaine
          +d per conversational turn. The second is algorithmic, answerin
          +g RQ3: the extension of the bot-play framework to a continuous
          + semantic action space with LLM verbalisation, evaluated withi
          +n that framework against the strongest non-learned and LLM-bas
          +ed baselines. The diagnostic findings already obtained (Chapte
          +r~\ref{ch:con}) — concerning reward signal-to-noise ratios, th
          +e necessity of separating preference polarity from semantic co
          +ntent in user-state representations, and the sample-efficiency
          + demands of continuous-action learning — directly inform the d
          +esign of both contributions.

● Everything is in motion. Here's the full picture:

  Machine verdict — no Colab needed ✅

  i7 (4C/8T), 16GB RAM, working PyTorch 2.2.2 + SBERT + FAISS + OpenAI SDK,
  MovieLens-25M and the cached SBERT embeddings on disk. The instrument models
  at this scale train in tens of minutes on CPU. One caution: only 27GB disk
  free, so no new large artefacts.

  How CASPER survives — in both papers

  - Paper 1 re-backronyms it: CASPER: Controlled Assessment of Strategic 
  Preference Elicitation in Recommendation — the testbed name.
  - Paper 2 keeps the original meaning: the continuous-action agent (CASPER-CA),
  evaluated on the CASPER testbed, with CASPER-V (verbal bot-play) as the
  no-training variant. The brand becomes the umbrella for your whole research
  programme — arguably better than one paper owning it.

  Full titles, draft abstracts, per-paper to-do lists, decision rules, and the
  day-by-day timeline are in C:\dev\phd\PAPERS_PLAN.md (committed). Timeline
  recalibrated to actual working speed: Paper 1 code+experiments days 1–3, draft
  day 4; Papers 2a+2b code, skeletons and reasoning docs days 5–8; HANDOFF.md 
  for Opus day 9; day 10 buffer. Nothing in the plan blocks on long waits —
  every training run goes to background.

  Paper 1 is already running

  Big discovery while digging: your January "paper config" checkpoints
  (onehot_paper_config.pt, concept_paper_config.pt — the CSMAI-19-style
  extrapolation models over a 187-item slate of movies+genres+actors+directors)
  are already polarity-aware by construction — disliked items are first-class
  inputs there. So M1 may not need retraining at all. I've written the formal
  acceptance test suite (casper/scripts/paper1/test_instrument.py: polarity
  overlap <30%, monotonicity, Star-Wars rating-flip, SNR) and it's running on 
  both checkpoints right now in the background. The 187-entity slate also gives
  Paper 1 a clean controlled design for free: every policy — random, greedy,
  LLM, bot-play — selects questions from the same fixed entity menu, so strategy
  is the only variable.

  When the tests finish I'll report the verdict and proceed straight to M2 (the
  harness) and M3 (the policies). Task list is live (8 tasks across both papers)
  — you can check progress anytime with /tasks.

● Day 1 status — ahead of plan. Here's where everything stands:

  Done this session

  - M2 harness: complete and smoke-tested. The CASPER testbed runs end-to-end:
  verifiable rule-based simulator (every answer auditable against ground truth),
  per-turn accuracy/BCE curves, bootstrap CIs, AUAC, hit-rate. Strict user
  separation: instrument trains on the 80% split, early-stops on the next 1,500
  users, and the testbed evaluates only on the ~11,400 users beyond that.
  - M3 policies: all six coded. Random, popularity, greedy info-gain (one-step
  expected entropy reduction, batched so it's fast), three LLM asker styles
  (vanilla / strategist-CoT / GATE-style interview, menu-constrained with
  parse-failure accounting), and the bot-play REINFORCE trainer faithful to your
  IJCNN method. LLM path smoke-tested live against the OpenAI API — works.
  - M4 runner: ready (run_benchmark.py, resumable, merges results across runs).

  The interesting science already emerging

  The acceptance tests rejected both January checkpoints — but for different,
  paper-worthy reasons:
  - The onehot model has genuine polarity (franchise flip test: 91.7% correct,
  e.g. disliking Star Wars IV drops the sequels ~20 ranks) but was trained only
  on full-profile inputs, so the few-reveals regime of an actual dialogue is
  out-of-distribution (single-attribute polarity only 68%; in the smoke test
  even greedy info-gain could barely move accuracy 0.711→0.722 in 5 turns).
  - The concept (SBERT) model is polarity-broken outright (16.7% on franchise
  flips — anti-correlated).

  This is Figure 1 of the paper: the measurement instrument, not the elicitation
  policy, was the binding constraint — and it retroactively explains your V1–V4
  failures.

  The fix is training now (background, epoch 7/60, ~45 min left): same
  architecture, one change — each training sample reveals only a random
  log-uniform subset of the profile (15% attribute-only episodes), making
  dialogue-regime inputs in-distribution.

  Next (auto-continues when training finishes)

  1. Re-run acceptance tests on instrument v2 → M1 verdict.
  2. Train the bot-play policy against v2 (~10 min).
  3. Full benchmark: free policies at 200 users, then LLM policies (~$5–15).

  Everything committed (c5dbcbb + follow-up). I'll pick up automatically when
  the training notification arrives.

      128 +def test_attribute_polarity_did(wrapper, items, attr_map):    
      129 +    """T1v3: difference-in-differences in score space.        
      130 +                                                              
      131 +    A disliked attribute may legitimately shift ALL prediction
          +s down (it                                                    
      132 +    signals a harsher rater overall); what polarity requires i
          +s that                                                        
      133 +    MATCHING movies move by more than non-matching ones:      
      134 +        DiD(e) = [mean_match(liked) - mean_match(disliked)]   
      135 +               - [mean_other(liked) - mean_other(disliked)]  >
          + 0                                                            
      136 +    """                                                       
      137 +    results = []                                              
      138 +    n_movies = wrapper.n_movies                               
      139 +    for attr_idx, movie_idxs in attr_map.items():             
      140 +        if len(movie_idxs) < 3:                               
      141 +            continue                                          
      142 +        liked = wrapper.predict([(attr_idx, 1.0)])            
      143 +        disliked = wrapper.predict([(attr_idx, 0.0)])         
      144 +        match = np.zeros(n_movies, dtype=bool)                
      145 +        match[movie_idxs] = True                              
      146 +        d_match = float(liked[match].mean() - disliked[match].
          +mean())                                                       
      147 +        d_other = float(liked[~match].mean() - disliked[~match
          +].mean())                                                     
      148 +        results.append({                                      
      149 +            'attribute': items[attr_idx][2],                  
      150 +            'type': items[attr_idx][0],                       
      151 +            'n_movies': len(movie_idxs),                      
      152 +            'delta_matching': d_match,                        
      153 +            'delta_other': d_other,                           
      154 +            'did': d_match - d_other,                         
      155 +            'correct': d_match > d_other,                     
      156 +        })                                                    
      157 +    frac = float(np.mean([r['correct'] for r in results])) if 
          +results else np.nan                                           
      158 +    mean_did = float(np.mean([r['did'] for r in results])) if 
          +results else np.nan                                           
      159 +    by_type = {}                                              
      160 +    for t in ('genre', 'actor', 'director'):                  
      161 +        sub = [r for r in results if r['type'] == t]          
      162 +        if sub:                                               
      163 +            by_type[t] = {'n': len(sub),                      
      164 +                          'frac_correct': float(np.mean([r['co
          +rrect'] for r in sub])),                                      
      165 +                          'mean_did': float(np.mean([r['did'] 
          +for r in sub]))}                                              
      166 +    worst = sorted(results, key=lambda r: r['did'])[:5]       
      167 +    return {                                                  
      168 +        'n_attributes_tested': len(results),                  
      169 +        'fraction_correct_direction': frac,                   
      170 +        'mean_did': mean_did,                                 
      171 +        'by_type': by_type,                                   
      172 +        'worst_attributes': worst,                            
      173 +        'pass': bool(frac >= 0.80),                           
      174 +    }                                                         
      175 +                                                              
      176 +

      179                    if it[0] == 'genre' and len(attr_map.get(i, 
           [])) >= 3]

● Everything you asked for is done, plus the science moved forward
  substantially:

  Your two questions

  1. Paper summaries: C:\dev\phd\PAPERS_PLAN.md — titles, draft abstracts, and
  to-do lists for all three (Paper 1 testbed, 2a CASPER-CA, 2b CASPER-V), with
  the decision rule for choosing between 2a/2b.
  2. Paper 1 full journal draft: written →
  C:\dev\phd\papers\paper1_casper\paper1_casper.tex (+ merged 74-entry
  references.bib, compiles standalone with pdflatex+bibtex). It's a complete
  long-format paper — abstract, introduction with the three contributions, full
  related work, a formal "measurement problem" section (including the
  sample-size arithmetic), testbed design, the instrument audit section with 
  today's real numbers already in it, all eight policies specified
  mathematically, results templates with the planned tables/figures, discussion
  scaffolding pre-written for both possible outcomes, and limitations. Only
  [RESULTS] markers remain to fill.

  Meanwhile, the instrument work took a fascinating turn

  The reveal-subset retrain (v2) fixed the out-of-distribution problem
  spectacularly at the combo level (liked-vs-disliked top-10 overlap collapsed
  from 43% → 0.7%) — but my new difference-in-differences test revealed its
  single-attribute response had collapsed into a pure rater-harshness signal:
  "dislikes Horror" in a slate of beloved classics mostly identifies harsh 
  raters, not horror-haters, so the model learned exactly that. The old model,
  conversely, has perfect genre polarity in score space (100% direction, DiD
  +0.111) but can't handle dialogue-regime inputs.

  The fix went into the label construction: attribute labels are now
  taste-relative (attribute mean vs the user's own overall mean, ±0.25 margin,
  ≥3 supporting movies, otherwise "unknown") — shared by the trainer, simulator,
  and tests from one function. Instrument v3 is training right now (~20 min);
  v2 is preserved as a paper ablation. This whole arc — full-profile OOD failure
  → harshness collapse → taste-relative labels — is Section 4.3 of the paper
  and it's already written in.

  When the training notification lands I'll run the acceptance suite on v3, then
  the bot-play training, then the benchmark. All committed (4eac55e and prior).

      329 +\section{A Case Study in Measurement Failure}                 
      330 +\label{sec:casestudy}                                         
      331 +                                                              
      332 +Before describing the testbed, we document the experience that
          + motivated                                                    
      333 +it: a reinforcement-learning research programme on elicitation
          + that stalled                                                 
      334 +for reasons that were ultimately about measurement, not learni
          +ng. We report                                                 
      335 +it both as evidence for the claims of Section~\ref{sec:problem
          +} and because                                                 
      336 +we believe the pattern is common but rarely published.        
      337 +                                                              
      338 +\subsection{Three Failed Training Campaigns}                  
      339 +                                                              
      340 +Building on \citet{makarova2024learning}, we attempted to trai
          +n an                                                          
      341 +elicitation policy with a continuous semantic action space, re
          +warded by                                                     
      342 +per-turn improvements in NDCG@10 of an LLM-based recommender o
          +ver a                                                         
      343 +candidate pool, against an LLM-simulated user. Three campaigns
          + failed in                                                    
      344 +sequence. The first produced \emph{negative} mean rewards     
      345 +($-0.25$ over the final ten episodes): the evaluation pool was
          + so saturated                                                 
      346 +with popular ground-truth items that the recommender's zero-kn
          +owledge                                                       
      347 +baseline was already high, and revealing genuine preferences f
          +requently                                                     
      348 +\emph{lowered} the metric. The second collapsed into repetitio
          +n once the                                                    
      349 +pool configuration was corrected. The third, with a within-epi
          +sode                                                          
      350 +similarity penalty, converged to a fixed question sequence del
          +ivered                                                        
      351 +identically to every user: a single concept accounted for 40\%
          + of all                                                       
      352 +second-turn questions, and only 35\% of 783 question selection
          +s were                                                        
      353 +unique across 90 episodes. At no point did any campaign learn 
      354 +user-adaptive questioning.                                    
      355 +                                                              
      356 +\subsection{Quantifying the Reward Channel}                   
      357 +                                                              
      358 +Post-hoc diagnostics located the failure in the measurement ch
          +annel rather                                                  
      359 +than the policy. Table~\ref{tab:pool_baseline} shows the zero-
          +knowledge                                                     
      360 +baseline of the NDCG-based reward as a function of two protoco
          +l parameters:                                                 
      361 +candidate pool size and the minimum number of ground-truth ite
          +ms required                                                   
      362 +per evaluation user. The configuration used in training (pool 
          +100, minimum                                                  
      363 +25 targets) yields a baseline of 0.52 --- with $\sim$39\% of t
          +he pool being                                                 
      364 +ground truth, popularity guessing scores well before any quest
          +ion is asked                                                  
      365 +--- leaving almost no headroom for elicitation to be rewarded.
      366 +

          +20. At that

          +ard an                                                        
      412 +individual training episode delivers is statistically indistin
          +guishable

          + fitting

          +ule, or

          +e repaired

      417 +                                                              
      418 +\subsection{Lessons}                                          
      419 +                                                              
      420 +The case study yields the design requirements of              
      421 +Section~\ref{sec:testbed}: (i) the measurement instrument must
          + be audited                                                   
      422 +\emph{before} policy work, against explicit acceptance criteri
          +a; (ii) the                                                   
      423 +simulated user must be verifiable, so that reward noise cannot
          + enter                                                        
      424 +through hallucinated or inconsistent answers; (iii) the per-tu
          +rn signal                                                     
      425 +and its dispersion must be measured and reported, because they
          + determine                                                    
      426 +both learnability and the user sample needed for evaluation; a
          +nd (iv) the                                                   
      427 +protocol parameters that Table~\ref{tab:pool_baseline} shows t
          +o dominate                                                    
      428 +the metric must be fixed and shared across all compared polici
          +es.

          +e had

          +hough the

          +t.

          +xplanation

          +ked'', the

      588 +but lowered \emph{every other movie even further}, so that in 
          +rank terms                                                    
      589 +the horror films \emph{rose} from ranks 39--93 into the top 6.
          + The model                                                    
      590 +had learned that the derived label ``dislikes Horror'' chiefly
          + identifies                                                   
      591 +a \emph{harsh rater}: in a slate composed of beloved classics 
      592 +(\emph{The Silence of the Lambs}, \emph{Alien}, \emph{The Shin
          +ing}), a                                                      
      593 +user whose mean rating of these films falls below the liking t
          +hreshold is,                                                  
      594 +overwhelmingly, a user who rates everything severely. The sign
          +al is real                                                    
      595 +and genuinely predictive of held-out ratings --- which is exac
          +tly why a                                                     
      596 +well-trained model learns it in preference to the much weaker 
      597 +attribute-specific taste signal. Under full-profile training t
          +his                                                           
      598 +shortcut is unavailable (dozens of co-revealed movie ratings a
          +lready pin                                                    
      599 +down the user's severity), which is why the original instrumen
          +t had                                                         
      600 +learned genuine attribute polarity instead.                   
      601 +                                                              
      602 +The repair therefore belongs in the \emph{label construction},
          + not the                                                      
      603 +model. We re-derive attribute ground truth \emph{taste-relativ
          +ely}: an                                                      
      604 +attribute is \textsf{liked} (\textsf{disliked}) only if the us
          +er's mean                                                     
      605 +rating over matching slate movies exceeds (falls below) \emph{
          +that user's                                                   
      606 +own overall slate mean} by a margin of 0.25, with at least thr
          +ee matching                                                   
      607 +rated movies; otherwise the attribute answers \textsf{unknown}
          +. Severity                                                    
      608 +is thereby removed from the attribute channel by construction,
          + on both                                                      
      609 +the training side and the simulator side (which derives answer
          +s from the                                                    
      610 +same labels). This also improves the simulator's face validity
          +: a user                                                      
      611 +who simply rates everything low is no longer described as ``di
          +sliking''                                                     
      612 +every genre. \results{v3 acceptance column in Table~\ref{tab:a
          +udit}; if                                                     
      613 +T1b still $<$80\%, report the auxiliary polarity-contrast loss
          + variant}                                                     
      614 +                                                              
      615 +The three-generation arc --- full-profile training fails the d
          +ialogue                                                       
      616 +regime; regime-correct training collapses onto rater severity;
          + severity                                                     
      617 +must be removed from the labels --- illustrates the paper's ce
          +ntral                                                         
      618 +methodological claim in miniature: every one of these failures
          + would have                                                   
      619 +been invisible in a whole-system evaluation, and each would ha
          +ve silently                                                   
      620 +distorted any policy comparison run on top of it.             
      621 +                                                              
      622  \subsection{Metrics}
      623  
      624  For each policy we report: the per-turn accuracy curve with bo
           otstrap 95\%

      622  \subsection{Metrics}
      623 +\label{sec:metrics}                                           
      624  
      624 -For each policy we report: the per-turn accuracy curve with bo
          -otstrap 95\%                                                  
      625 -confidence intervals over users; final accuracy at the turn bu
          -dget; AUAC                                                    
      626 -(mean accuracy over turns, rewarding early gains); BCE traject
          -ories                                                         
      627 -(Eq.~\ref{eq:acc} with log loss); and \emph{hit rate}, the fra
          -ction of                                                      
      628 -questions landing on entities the user can answer --- a diagno
          -stic                                                          
      629 -separating ``asks about things people know'' from ``asks about
          - informative                                                  
      630 -things''. For LLM policies we additionally report parse-failur
          -e rates;                                                      
      631 -failed parses fall back to a random selection and are counted,
          - so                                                           
      632 -formatting unreliability degrades the LLM policies' scores exa
          -ctly as it                                                    
      633 -would degrade a deployed system.                              
      625 +\paragraph{Primary.} The per-turn accuracy curve              
      626 +$(\mathrm{Acc}_0, \ldots, \mathrm{Acc}_T)$ (Eq.~\ref{eq:acc}),
          + averaged                                                     
      627 +over users with bootstrap 95\% confidence intervals; \emph{fin
          +al accuracy}                                                  
      628 +$\mathrm{Acc}_T$; and \emph{AUAC}, the mean of the curve over 
          +turns, which                                                  
      629 +rewards front-loaded information gain --- two policies with eq
          +ual final                                                     
      630 +accuracy differ in AUAC if one achieves its gains earlier. BCE
      631 +trajectories are reported alongside as the smooth counterpart 
          +of accuracy.                                                  
      632  
      633 +\paragraph{Behavioural diagnostics.} Four quantities decompose
          + \emph{why}                                                   
      634 +a policy scores as it does:                                   
      635 +\begin{description}                                           
      636 +  \item[Hit rate.] The fraction of questions answered other th
          +an                                                            
      637 +  \textsf{unknown}. Separates ``asks about things this user ca
          +n answer''                                                    
      638 +  from ``asks about informative things''; the popularity basel
          +ine                                                           
      639 +  maximises it by construction.                               
      640 +  \item[Redundancy rate.] The fraction of questions whose answ
          +er was                                                        
      641 +  already inferable: the instrument's pre-question prediction 
          +for the                                                       
      642 +  asked entity exceeded $0.8$ (or fell below $0.2$) and the an
          +swer agreed.                                                  
      643 +  A redundant question is locally safe but strategically waste
          +d.                                                            
      644 +  \item[Question-type mix.] The distribution of question targe
          +ts over                                                       
      645 +  \{genre, actor, director, movie\} as a function of turn numb
          +er ---                                                        
      646 +  whether a policy opens broad and refines, or interrogates it
          +ems                                                           
      647 +  immediately.                                                
      648 +  \item[Coverage.] The entropy of the policy's question distri
          +bution                                                        
      649 +  across users at each turn: a fixed-sequence policy has zero 
          +coverage                                                      
      650 +  entropy at every turn (every user gets the same question), a
          + fully                                                        
      651 +  user-adaptive policy a high one. This is the metric under wh
          +ich the                                                       
      652 +  fixed-policy collapse of Section~\ref{sec:casestudy} is visi
          +ble at a                                                      
      653 +  glance.                                                     
      654 +\end{description}                                             
      655 +                                                              
      656 +\paragraph{LLM accounting.} For LLM policies we report parse-f
          +ailure rates                                                  
      657 +and API cost. Failed parses fall back to a random selection an
          +d are                                                         
      658 +counted against the policy, so formatting unreliability degrad
          +es LLM                                                        
      659 +scores exactly as it would degrade a deployed system.         
      660 +                                                              
      661 +\paragraph{Statistical reporting.} All policy contrasts are re
          +ported as                                                     
      662 +paired differences over the identical user set with bootstrap 
          +CIs;                                                          
      663 +the instrument's measured per-reveal gain and between-user dis
          +persion                                                       
      664 +(T4) accompany every table so that readers can verify the cont
          +rasts are                                                     
      665 +resolvable at the sample size used (Section~\ref{sec:snr_theor
          +y}).                                                          
      666 +

      876 +\section{LLM Questioner Prompts}                              
      877 +\label{app:prompts}                                           
      878 +                                                              
      879 +The three prompt styles of Section~\ref{sec:policies}, verbati
          +m. All use                                                    
      880 +temperature 0 and a 150-token completion limit; the menu of re
          +maining                                                       
      881 +entities and the dialogue transcript are appended to each requ
          +est.                                                          
      882 +                                                              
      883 +\subsection*{A.1 Vanilla}                                     
      884 +\begin{verbatim}                                              
      885 +You are a movie recommendation assistant interviewing a user t
          +o                                                             
      886 +learn their taste. At each step you choose ONE entity to ask a
          +bout                                                          
      887 +(whether they like it), from a fixed menu. Reply with the exac
          +t                                                             
      888 +entity name from the menu, and nothing else.                  
      889 +\end{verbatim}                                                
      890 +                                                              
      891 +\subsection*{A.2 Strategist}                                  
      892 +\begin{verbatim}                                              
      893 +You are an expert preference-elicitation strategist for a movi
          +e                                                             
      894 +recommender. Your goal: in as few questions as possible, learn
      895 +enough about this user's taste to predict their ratings for 10
          +0                                                             
      896 +popular movies. At each step you choose ONE entity from a fixe
          +d                                                             
      897 +menu.                                                         
      898 +                                                              
      899 +Think strategically before answering:                         
      900 +- Broad attributes (genres) split the space fastest early on. 
      901 +- Follow up on what you have learned: refine within liked area
          +s,                                                            
      902 +  confirm dislikes.                                           
      903 +- Questions the user can't answer ('unknown') are wasted turns
          +;                                                             
      904 +  popular entities are safer.                                 
      905 +- Avoid redundant questions whose answer you can already infer
          +.                                                             
      906 +                                                              
      907 +First think step by step in at most 50 words, then output the 
      908 +chosen entity name on the final line in the exact form:       
      909 +CHOICE: <entity name>                                         
      910 +\end{verbatim}                                                
      911 +                                                              
      912 +\subsection*{A.3 GATE-style}                                  
      913 +\begin{verbatim}                                              
      914 +You are conducting a free-form preference interview, but your 
      915 +questions must be realised by selecting ONE entity from a fixe
          +d                                                             
      916 +menu (the system asks 'do you like <entity>?'). Run the interv
          +iew                                                           
      917 +the way an expert interviewer would: open with broad probes, t
          +est                                                           
      918 +edge cases of what you believe, and probe contrasts (if they l
          +ike                                                           
      919 +X, check a nearby Y that would distinguish two hypotheses abou
          +t                                                             
      920 +their taste). Output the chosen entity name on the final line 
          +as:                                                           
      921 +CHOICE: <entity name>                                         
      922 +\end{verbatim}

      924 +\section{Instrument Training and Audit Detail}                
      925 +\label{app:instrument}                                        
      926 +                                                              
      927 +\subsection*{B.1 Training configuration}                      
      928 +Architecture: item embedding (dimension $\lfloor N/2 \rfloor =
          + 93$)                                                         
      929 +concatenated with the rating one-hot, single-layer LSTM (hidde
          +n 93),                                                        
      930 +dense bottleneck, single-head attention, linear head over the 
          +slate;                                                        
      931 +193{,}535 parameters. Optimiser Adam, learning rate $10^{-3}$,
          + batch 32,                                                    
      932 +masked BCE over rated targets at every sequence position, earl
          +y stopping                                                    
      933 +on validation loss (patience 10). Reveal-subset augmentation: 
          +per sample,                                                   
      934 +$k \sim \exp(\mathcal{U}(0, \ln(n_{\mathrm{rated}}+1)))$ revea
          +led items;                                                    
      935 +with probability 0.15 the candidate reveal set is restricted t
          +o                                                             
      936 +attributes. Training population: 12{,}000 users of the 80\% pa
          +rtition                                                       
      937 +(of 64{,}639 eligible); model selection on 1{,}500 held-out us
          +ers.                                                          
      938 +Wall-clock on a 4-core laptop CPU: $\approx$45--60 s per epoch
          +,                                                             
      939 +early-stopped at \results{epoch} ($\approx$\results{} minutes 
          +total).                                                       
      940 +\results{training curve figure}                               
      941 +                                                              
      942 +\subsection*{B.2 Franchise flip detail}                       
      943 +\results{per-franchise table: anchor, related movies, rank mov
          +ement under                                                   
      944 +liked vs disliked anchor, for the accepted instrument --- the 
          +v1 audit                                                      
      945 +measured 8 franchises with slate support (Star Wars, Matrix, T
          +oy Story,                                                     
      946 +Lord of the Rings, Terminator, Back to the Future, Jurassic Pa
          +rk,                                                           
      947 +Godfather, et al.)}                                           
      948 +                                                              
      949 +\subsection*{B.3 Attribute label statistics}                  
      950 +\results{coverage: fraction of users carrying a non-unknown la
          +bel per                                                       
      951 +attribute under absolute vs taste-relative derivation; the tas
          +te-relative                                                   
      952 +scheme abstains where the absolute scheme fabricated severity-
          +driven                                                        
      953 +labels}                                                       
      954 +

  ---
  Now your other question — the harsh, unbiased review. Here's my honest referee
  report on this paper as currently scoped:

  Novelty: genuine but narrow (6.5/10). The core idea — audit the measurement
  instrument before benchmarking elicitation, and isolate strategy behind a
  shared action interface — is real and verified-unclaimed in the literature.
  The instrument-audit arc (OOD → harshness collapse → label fix) is the most
  original material; I haven't seen the rater-severity confound documented
  anywhere. But "a benchmark + baselines" is a well-trodden paper shape, and
  reviewers grade those on scale and adoption potential.

  Where a tough reviewer will hit you, in order of pain:
  1. Scale. One domain, one dataset, a 187-entity slate over the 100 most 
  popular movies. Top venues expect the CRS standard triple (MovieLens + Yelp +
  LastFM) or at least two domains. Worse, the blockbuster slate compresses the
  dynamic range — prior accuracy is already ~71%, so the benchmark measures
  gains in a narrow band. "Toy testbed" is the one-word rejection risk.
  2. The menu constraint cuts both ways. Forcing LLMs to pick from your entity
  menu is what makes the comparison controlled — but a reviewer will correctly
  note you've measured LLMs at your game, not theirs (free-form questioning). If
  the headline is "LLMs are weak strategists", they'll call it a strawman.
  Mitigation exists and is cheap: add a free-form LLM arm whose questions are
  embedding-mapped to the nearest entity — which conveniently is also the bridge
  to Paper 2a.
  3. The instrument is a 190k-parameter LSTM. "Instrument validity" claims
  invite "why not a stronger reference model?" — at minimum a
  matrix-factorization comparison is expected.
  4. Simulator realism. Deterministic label lookup is a deliberate, well-argued
  regression from iEvaLM — but it is a regression in the direction the field
  moved, and some reviewers weight realism over verifiability.
  5. No published CRS system among the policies. Without a UNICORN-style
  entropy-attribute baseline, the claim "the lineage has never been compared
  like-for-like" rings slightly hollow — you compare categories of policy, not
  the published systems.

  Venue calibration (honest): as-is — RecSys short paper ~50/50 if the results
  are crisp; UMAP or ECIR full plausible; CIKM full risky; SIGIR/KDD no.
  Workshop (KaRS/IntRS) near-certain. One tier higher (ECIR/CIKM full,
  comfortably) costs roughly: a second dataset or a 500-movie slate, the
  free-form LLM arm, an MF reference instrument, and one published-lineage
  baseline — maybe 1–2 extra weeks, mostly compute we have.

  Value: higher for the PhD than for the field, and that's fine. For the thesis
  it's near-ideal: it converts three failed training campaigns into evidence,
  produces the infrastructure Paper 2 stands on, and is publishable somewhere
  with high confidence. For the field, its citation fate depends on whether
  anyone adopts the testbed — single-group benchmarks usually get cited for
  their findings (the LLM-vs-greedy number, the harshness confound) rather than
  used, so make those findings the headline, not the artifact.

  My recommendation: run the benchmark as planned this week, and if the results
  are interesting, spend the marginal week on weaknesses #1 and #2 before
  submission — they're the difference between "nice workshop paper" and "solid
  conference paper". Meanwhile instrument v3 should be done training shortly;
  the moment it passes acceptance, results start flowing.

      124 +class SetInstrumentWrapper:                                   
      125 +    """Same interface as InstrumentWrapper, for the v5 set-enc
          +oder model."""                                                
      126 +                                                              
      127 +    def __init__(self, model, n_items, n_movies, max_reveal=60
          +):                                                            
      128 +        self.model = model                                    
      129 +        self.n_items = n_items                                
      130 +        self.n_movies = n_movies                              
      131 +        self.max_reveal = max_reveal                          
      132 +                                                              
      133 +    def _forward(self, revealed_list):                        
      134 +        b = len(revealed_list)                                
      135 +        L = max(1, max((len(r) for r in revealed_list), defaul
          +t=1))                                                         
      136 +        idx = torch.zeros(b, L, dtype=torch.long)             
      137 +        pol = torch.zeros(b, L, dtype=torch.long)             
      138 +        pad = torch.ones(b, L, dtype=torch.bool)              
      139 +        for i, revealed in enumerate(revealed_list):          
      140 +            for j, (e, p) in enumerate(revealed[:self.max_reve
          +al]):                                                         
      141 +                idx[i, j] = int(e)                            
      142 +                pol[i, j] = 1 if p >= 0.5 else 0              
      143 +                pad[i, j] = False                             
      144 +        with torch.no_grad():                                 
      145 +            return torch.sigmoid(self.model(idx, pol, pad)).nu
          +mpy()

      154 +        out = self._forward(revealed_list)

● Everything's tracked and committed; v4 finishes in ~5 minutes, and v5 (the
  set-encoder) launches right after. Now your question — what Paper 1 is 
  actually trying to achieve, in plain terms:

  The one-sentence version

  Paper 1 builds the first fair measuring stick for the question "who asks 
  better questions — an algorithm, a formula, or ChatGPT?" — and then uses it to
  actually answer that question.

  The slightly longer version

  Every conversational recommender lives or dies by what it asks the user. Your
  whole PhD is about learning to ask well. But here's the embarrassing situation
  in the field, which our literature verification confirmed: nobody can 
  currently measure asking-skill on its own. Published systems are evaluated as
  package deals — questions + recommender + language generation all tangled
  together — so when System A beats System B, you can't tell whether A asks
  smarter questions or just has a better recommender attached. And the field's
  loudest current claim — "LLMs are bad at strategic elicitation" (the claim
  your thesis gap rests on!) — has literally never been measured. It lives in
  papers' motivation sections.

  Paper 1 fixes this with three moves:

  1. A fixed "measuring instrument." One frozen recommender model converts
  answers into predictions for every competing questioner alike — so the
  recommender can't be the reason one policy wins. The twist that emerged from
  our own work this week: the instrument itself can be silently broken (can't
  tell likes from dislikes, can't handle short dialogues, mistakes "harsh rater"
  for "hates horror") — so the paper defines acceptance tests an instrument
  must pass first, and documents five generations of instructive failures. This
  audit is itself a novel contribution.
  2. A verifiable simulated user. Answers come from real MovieLens users' actual
  ratings by lookup — every answer auditable, zero hallucination — because when
  you're measuring information-per-question, a simulator that makes things up
  corrupts exactly what you're measuring.
  3. One shared question menu. Every contestant — random, popularity, a textbook
  information-gain formula, three flavours of prompted GPT, and your published
  IJCNN bot-play policy — picks questions from the same 187 entities. Strategy
  becomes the only difference.

  Then the benchmark answers, with confidence intervals: do prompted LLMs 
  actually ask strategically? Whatever the answer, it's publishable — if LLMs
  lose badly, you've quantified the gap your learned-policy research (Paper 2)
  exists to close, with the training signal and evaluation already built; if
  LLMs match the formula, you've overturned the field's motivating assumption,
  which is arguably a bigger headline.

  Why it's the right first paper for you specifically

  - It converts your three failed training years into its motivating case study
  (they become evidence, not waste).
  - It reuses ~90% of CASPER's infrastructure and keeps the CASPER name.
  - It requires no risky RL — only supervised training and API calls.
  - And Paper 2 stands on top of it: same simulator, same instrument, same
  baselines — CASPER-CA just becomes one more contestant on a leaderboard the
  world already accepted.

      231 +def test_multifranchise_did(wrapper, items):                  
      232 +    """T3v3: franchise flip in score space (DiD vs non-franchi
          +se movies)."""                                                
      233 +    title_by_idx = {i: it[2] for i, it in enumerate(items) if 
          +it[0] == 'movie'}                                             
      234 +    n_movies = wrapper.n_movies                               
      235 +    checks, details = [], {}                                  
      236 +    for fname, pat in FRANCHISE_PATTERNS.items():             
      237 +        members = [i for i, t in title_by_idx.items() if re.se
          +arch(pat, str(t))]                                            
      238 +        if len(members) < 2:                                  
      239 +            continue                                          
      240 +        anchor, related = members[0], members[1:]             
      241 +        liked = wrapper.predict([(anchor, 1.0)])              
      242 +        disliked = wrapper.predict([(anchor, 0.0)])           
      243 +        rel_mask = np.zeros(n_movies, dtype=bool)             
      244 +        rel_mask[related] = True                              
      245 +        other_mask = ~rel_mask                                
      246 +        other_mask[anchor] = False                            
      247 +        d_rel = float(liked[rel_mask].mean() - disliked[rel_ma
          +sk].mean())

          +her_mask].mean())

      250 +        details[fname] = {'anchor': title_by_idx[anchor],     
      251 +                          'did': d_rel - d_other,             
      252 +                          'delta_related': d_rel, 'delta_other
          +': d_other}                                                   
      253 +    frac = float(np.mean(checks)) if checks else np.nan       
      254 +    return {

      256 +        'fraction_correct_direction': frac,                   
      257 +        'details': details,

● Here's where everything stands:

  Instrument race — v5 (set-encoder) is winning decisively

  ┌────────────────────┬──────┬─────┬─────┬────────────┬─────────────────┐
  │        Gate        │ v1   │ v2  │ v3  │    v4      │ v5 set-encoder  │
  │                    │ LSTM │ +RS │ +TR │ warm-start │                 │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼─────┼─────┼────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Genre polarity     │ ✅   │ ❌  │ ❌  │ ✅ 100%    │ ✅ pass         │
  │ (DiD ≥80%)         │ 100% │ 37% │ 75% │            │                 │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼─────┼─────┼────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Franchise flips    │ ❌   │ ❌* │ ❌  │ ❌ 62%     │ ✅ 75%          │
  │ (DiD)              │      │     │ 37% │            │                 │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼─────┼─────┼────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Liked/disliked     │ ✅   │ ✅  │ ✅  │ ✅         │ ✅              │
  │ separation         │      │     │     │            │                 │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼─────┼─────┼────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ Dialogue regime    │ ❌   │ ✅  │ ✅  │ ✅         │ ✅              │
  │ in-distribution    │      │     │     │            │                 │
  ├────────────────────┼──────┼─────┼─────┼────────────┼─────────────────┤
  │ All gates          │ ❌   │ ❌  │ ❌  │ ❌         │ ✅              │
  │                    │      │     │     │            │ (mid-training!) │
  └────────────────────┴──────┴─────┴─────┴────────────┴─────────────────┘

  And the headline number: v5's validation loss is 0.48 vs 0.61 for v4 on the
  same labels — the set-encoder isn't just passing polarity gates, it's a
  substantially better predictor. The architectural diagnosis (1–3 reveals
  drowning among 184 "unseen" tokens in the LSTM's 93-dim recurrence) was right,
  and your instinct to keep iterating instead of time-boxing was too.

  It's at epoch 30/80 and still improving (~30s/epoch now — the set encoder is
  also 2× faster to train). Probably 20–40 minutes to early-stop.

  What happened since the last update

  - v4 (warm-start fine-tune): partial success — kept v1's perfect genre
  polarity, fixed the regime, but franchise flips stayed marginal. Useful as a
  paper data point.
  - I caught and fixed a test artifact: the franchise test was rank-based and
  suffered the same global-shift confound as the attribute test — both now use
  difference-in-differences with a noise floor, and the gates are the redefined
  ones from task #12 (documented rationale, not gate-shopping: every change is
  justified by an identified measurement artifact).
  - Test power note: the top-100 slate contains only 8 multi-movie franchises —
  yet another datum for the larger-slate ablation (#14).

  Next, automatically

  When v5's training notification arrives: full acceptance suite including
  monotonicity/SNR → if it holds (everything so far says it will), v5 is the 
  instrument → bot-play policy trains against it (~15 min) → free-policy
  benchmark (~15 min) → LLM benchmark (~$5–10). Realistically you'll have the
  first real leaderboard today.

  Day-2 scorecard vs plan: M1 took longer than planned (five generations instead
  of two) but produced the paper's richest section in exchange; M2 done; M3
  code done; the draft is ~15pp prose with figures pipeline tested. Still on
  schedule overall.

● Web Search("OpenAI API pricing per million tokens June 2026 GPT-5
            gpt-4o-mini")
  ⎿  Did 1 search in 10s
● Here's the model plan and the real costs (verified against current pricing,
  June 2026):

  Current OpenAI lineup & prices (per 1M tokens, in/out)

  - GPT-5.5 (flagship): $5.00 / $30.00
  - GPT-5.4 (previous flagship): $2.50 / $15.00
  - GPT-5.4-nano (budget): $0.20 / $1.25
  - gpt-4o-mini (legacy budget, what's wired in now): $0.15 / $0.60

  Cost arithmetic for our benchmark

  One full arm = 200 users × 15 turns = 3,000 calls per prompt style; ~1,400
  input tokens/call (the 187-entity menu dominates), 15–120 output tokens. So
  per style: ~4.2M input tokens.

  ┌────────────────────┬──────────────┬──────────────────────────┬─────────┐
  │        Tier        │    Model     │          Scope           │  Est.   │
  │                    │              │                          │  cost   │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Small              │ GPT-5.4-nano │ full grid (3 styles ×    │ ~$4     │
  │                    │              │ 200 users)               │         │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Mid                │ GPT-5.4-mini │ full grid                │ ~$8–12  │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Flagship           │ GPT-5.5      │ 2 styles (best +         │ ~$55–65 │
  │                    │              │ strategist) × 200 users  │         │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Flagship-lite      │ GPT-5.4      │ 2 styles × 200           │ ~$25–30 │
  │ alternative        │              │                          │         │
  ├────────────────────┼──────────────┼──────────────────────────┼─────────┤
  │ Legacy anchor      │ gpt-4o-mini  │ full grid                │ ~$2.50  │
  │ (optional)         │              │                          │         │
  └────────────────────┴──────────────┴──────────────────────────┴─────────┘

  Recommended package: nano grid + mini grid + GPT-5.5 two styles ≈ $75–90 
  total. Minimum defensible: nano grid + GPT-5.5 one style ≈ $35. I won't launch
  anything above the ~$5 nano tier without your go-ahead.

  Why this ladder is the right design, not just fairness insurance

  Your instinct upgrades the science: instead of one "LLMs score X" row, we get
  a capability-scaling curve — does strategic questioning improve from nano →
  mini → flagship? That directly answers the unfairness objection and is a more
  citable finding either way: "strategy emerges with scale" or "even flagships
  don't out-ask a greedy formula" are both headlines. The harsh-review concern
  B4 ("only tested a small model") dies completely.

  Two notes:
  1. Billing goes to the OpenAI key in casper/.env — confirm you're happy with
  ~$75–90 on it (and that it has GPT-5.5 access / sufficient rate limits; nano
  runs will verify the plumbing first).
  2. If you want provider diversity (a Claude model row would look good to
  reviewers), I'd need an Anthropic API key — optional, similar cost at the
  Sonnet tier.

  Meanwhile v5 is still training (epoch ~35/80, val still improving). The
  benchmark order remains: free policies first ($0), then nano ($4), then your
  call on the bigger tiers.

  Sources: OpenAI API Pricing (https://openai.com/api/pricing/),
  pricepertoken.com OpenAI pricing
  (https://pricepertoken.com/pricing-page/provider/openai), costgoat.com OpenAI
  calculator (https://costgoat.com/pricing/openai-api)

● First real leaderboard — and your method wins it, significantly

  CASPER benchmark: 200 held-out users × 15 turns, accepted v5 instrument, all
